COLLEGE OF ARTS AND SCIENCES (CAS) — Psychology (major inferred), 4 years
PATHFIT 1 — Movement Competency Training (MCT)
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 2 Basic movement competency and fitness training. Students learn proper form and baseline fitness assessment. This course lays the groundwork for the subsequent PATHFit courses.
GEC 101 — Understanding the Self
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines the self through psychological, sociological, anthropological, and philosophical lenses. Students explore identity formation and how various disciplines frame human experience. The course encourages self-reflection as a foundation for personal growth throughout college life.
GEC 104 — Mathematics in the Modern World
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Presents mathematics as a tool for understanding patterns, reasoning, and decision-making. Covers statistics, logic, and applied mathematical reasoning. The course is designed to make math relevant to everyday and professional situations rather than purely abstract computation.
P.I. 100 — The Life and Works of Jose Rizal
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Studies Rizal's life, writings (Noli Me Tangere, El Filibusterismo), and his role in Philippine nationalism. Students analyze his lasting legacy in shaping national identity. The course is mandated for all Philippine college students under Republic Act 1425.
GEC ELEC 1 — Living in the IT Era
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Introduces students to digital literacy, information technology tools, and their impact on modern life. Students explore online safety, digital communication, and technology's role in society. The course builds practical tech competency for academic and everyday use.
NSTP 1 — CWTS 1 / ROTC 1
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: (3) | Prerequisite for: NSTP 2 The Civic Welfare Training Service (or ROTC) component of the National Service Training Program. Students engage in community-based service or military training activities. It fulfills part of the mandatory national service requirement for college students.
BC PSY 1 — Introduction to Psychology
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Introduces the fundamental concepts, theories, and subfields of psychology as a science. Students learn about major psychological perspectives and research approaches. This course establishes the conceptual foundation for the entire psychology curriculum.
KOMFIL — Kontekstwalisadong Komunikasyon sa Filipino
CAS | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: FILDIS Develops Filipino communication skills in contextualized academic and professional settings. Students practice discipline-specific Filipino registers. The course strengthens the ability to express technical and academic ideas in the national language.
GEC 102 — Readings in Philippine History
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines Philippine history through primary source documents. Students learn to read and interpret original historical texts. This approach builds critical thinking skills that go beyond memorizing dates and events.
FILDIS — Filipino sa Iba't Ibang Disiplina
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: KOMFIL Explores the use of Filipino across academic disciplines. Students learn discipline-specific Filipino terminology. The course reinforces the role of Filipino as a medium for technical and scholarly communication.
GEC ELEC 2 — Great Books
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Introduces students to influential literary and philosophical works that have shaped human thought. Students engage in close reading and critical discussion of classic texts. The course cultivates broad intellectual literacy alongside psychology-specific coursework.
NSTP 2 — CWTS 2 / ROTC 2 / LTS 2
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: (3) | Prerequisite: NSTP 1 The second National Service Training Program course, continuing from NSTP 1. Students typically implement a community project or continue military/literacy training. This course completes the two-part NSTP civic service requirement.
BC PSY 2 — Psychological Statistics
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: BC PSY 1 Covers statistical methods used to analyze psychological data, including descriptive and inferential statistics. Students learn to apply statistical software and interpret research findings. This course is essential preparation for the research courses later in the program.
NS ELEC 1 — General Biology
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) Covers foundational biological concepts including cell structure, genetics, and organismal biology. Students conduct laboratory work alongside lecture material. This course supports the physiological psychology coursework later in the program.
PATHFit 2 — Exercise-based Fitness Activities
CAS | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PATHFit 1 Continues the PATHFit series with structured exercise routines. Students track and improve personal fitness metrics. It builds directly on the movement foundations established in PATHFit 1.
GEC 105 — Purposive Communication
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Develops writing and speaking skills for academic and professional purposes. Students practice communication across varied genres and contexts. It prepares students for the kinds of writing and presentations expected in the workplace.
PE 3 — Individual/Dual Sports Games
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PE 2 Covers individual and dual sports such as badminton, table tennis, or similar activities. Students develop specific sport skills and strategies. This course continues the physical education sequence beyond the general PATHFit track.
PSY 2 — Experimental Psychology
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: BC PSY (incomplete in source) Introduces experimental design and methodology as applied to psychological research. Students design and conduct simple experiments while learning to control for confounding variables. This course builds directly on the statistics foundation from Psychological Statistics.
LIT 1 — The Tradition of Fiction
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: LIT 2 Examines fiction as a literary form across different periods and traditions. Students analyze narrative techniques and thematic development in prose works. The course develops literary analysis skills relevant to understanding human experience and behavior.
NS ELEC 2 — Principles of Chemistry
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: NS ELEC 1 (incomplete in source) Covers fundamental chemistry concepts including atomic structure, chemical bonding, and reactions. Students conduct laboratory experiments alongside lecture material. This course supports later coursework in physiological psychology.
GEC 106 — Art Appreciation
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Introduces students to visual, performing, and literary arts. Students explore art across historical periods and cultural contexts. The course cultivates aesthetic awareness alongside the program's scientific coursework.
GE ELECT 3 — Gender and Society
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines gender roles, identities, and inequalities within social and cultural contexts. Students critically analyze how gender shapes institutions, relationships, and opportunities. The course fosters awareness of gender-related issues relevant to psychological practice.
PSY 1 — Developmental Psychology
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: BC PSY (incomplete in source) Studies human development across the lifespan, from infancy through late adulthood. Students learn theories of cognitive, social, and emotional development at each life stage. This course underlies much of the later coursework on personality and clinical psychology.
PATHFit 3 — Choice of Dance, Sports, Martial Arts, Group Exercise, Outdoor and Adventure Activities
CAS | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PATHFit 2 Lets students choose a specialized physical activity track based on personal interest. Emphasizes sustained engagement in a chosen discipline. It gives students more autonomy in shaping their physical education path.
GEC 107 — Science, Technology and Society
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines the interplay between scientific advancement, technology, and societal change. Students critically assess technology's ethical and social implications. The course situates psychological practice within a broader technological and societal context.
DALUMATFIL — Dalumat Ng/Sa Filipino
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: FILDIS Explores Filipino thought, concepts, and intellectual traditions. Students examine how Filipino ideas and worldviews are constructed and expressed. This course builds on the Filipino-across-disciplines foundation from FILDIS.
GEC 108 — Ethics
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Introduces ethical theories and frameworks for moral reasoning. Students apply ethical analysis to real-world dilemmas. It provides groundwork for ethical practice in psychological research and counseling later in the program.
GEC 103 — The Contemporary World
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines globalization and its economic, political, and cultural dimensions. Students analyze how global interconnectedness shapes local realities. The course encourages a broader, more critical view of current world affairs.
LIT 2 — The Tradition of Poetry
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: LIT 1 Examines poetry as a literary form across different periods and traditions. Students analyze poetic devices, structure, and thematic content. This course continues the literary analysis skills built in The Tradition of Fiction.
PSY 3 — Theories of Personality
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: BC PSY (incomplete in source) Covers major theoretical frameworks for understanding human personality, including psychodynamic, humanistic, and trait-based approaches. Students learn to apply these theories to real behavioral case studies. This course builds directly on the developmental psychology foundation.
PSY 4 — Field Methods in Psychology
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Introduces field-based research methods used to study behavior in naturalistic settings. Students practice observation, interviewing, and data collection techniques outside the laboratory. This course complements the experimental methods learned in Experimental Psychology.
PATHFit 4 — Choice of Dance, Sports, Martial Arts, Group Exercise, Outdoor and Adventure Activities
CAS | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PATHFit 3 The culminating PATHFit course, continuing the student's chosen physical activity specialization. Emphasizes sustained fitness practice as a lifestyle habit. It completes the PATHFit physical education requirement.
PSY 5 — Physiological/Biological Psychology
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines the biological bases of behavior, including brain structure, neurotransmission, and the nervous system. Students learn how physiological processes underlie cognition and emotion. This course draws on the biology and chemistry foundation from earlier science electives.
PSY 6 — Abnormal Psychology
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Studies psychological disorders, their classification, causes, and treatment approaches. Students learn diagnostic criteria and explore case studies of various mental health conditions. This course builds on personality theory and developmental psychology foundations.
PSY 7 — Research in Psychology 1
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Guides students through the early stages of designing a formal psychological research study. Students develop a research proposal grounded in prior coursework on methodology and statistics. This course is the first half of a two-part research sequence.
PSY 8 — Sikolohiyang Filipino
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines psychology through an indigenous Filipino lens, exploring culturally-specific psychological concepts and frameworks. Students critically compare Western and Filipino approaches to understanding behavior. The course grounds psychological practice in local cultural context.
PSY 9 — Psychological Assessment
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Covers principles and methods of psychological testing, including personality inventories and cognitive assessments. Students learn to administer, score, and interpret standardized tests. This course prepares students for later coursework in specialized assessment seminars.
NS ELEC 3 — General Zoology
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: NS ELEC (incomplete in source) Covers the classification, anatomy, and behavior of animal organisms. Students conduct laboratory work examining various animal systems. This course extends the biological science foundation relevant to physiological psychology.
FL 1 — Basic Foreign Language
CAS | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: FL 2 Introduces foundational vocabulary, grammar, and conversational skills in a foreign language. Students practice basic reading, writing, and speaking exercises. This course lays the groundwork for advanced language study in the following semester.
PSY 10 — Research in Psychology 2
CAS | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source, likely PSY 7) Continues the research sequence, guiding students through data collection, analysis, and completion of their research study. Students finalize and present their psychological research project. This course completes the two-part research methodology sequence.
PSY ELEC 1 — Introduction to Clinical Psychology
CAS | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Introduces the field of clinical psychology, including diagnosis, therapeutic approaches, and the clinician's role. Students explore foundational clinical concepts and case formulation. This elective connects directly to the later clinical practicum.
PSY ELEC 2 — Psychology for Exceptional Children
CAS | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines the psychological and developmental needs of children with disabilities or special needs. Students learn about various exceptionalities and appropriate intervention approaches. This course extends developmental psychology concepts to specialized populations.
NS ELEC 4 — Human Anatomy and Physiology
CAS | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 5 (Lec 3 / Lab 2) | Prerequisite: NS ELEC (incomplete in source) Covers the structure and function of human body systems, with emphasis on the nervous and endocrine systems. Students conduct laboratory work examining anatomical structures. This course deepens the biological foundation for physiological psychology.
FL 2 — Advanced Foreign Language
CAS | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: FL 1 Builds on basic foreign language skills with more advanced grammar, vocabulary, and conversational practice. Students engage in more complex reading and communication tasks. This course completes the two-part foreign language requirement.
PRACTICUM 1 — Clinical Setting
CAS | Year 3, Inter-Semester | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY courses (incomplete in source) Places students in a supervised clinical setting to apply psychological theory and assessment skills in practice. Students gain direct exposure to real client interactions under professional supervision. This practicum bridges classroom learning with hands-on clinical experience.
PSY 11 — Cognitive Psychology
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines mental processes including perception, memory, language, and problem-solving. Students learn cognitive models and how they explain everyday thinking and decision-making. This course adds a cognitive science dimension to the psychology curriculum.
PSY 12 — Industrial/Organizational Psychology
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Applies psychological principles to workplace settings, covering topics like employee motivation, organizational behavior, and personnel selection. Students learn how psychology informs human resource practices. This course opens career pathways into I/O psychology roles.
PSY 13 — Social Psychology
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Studies how individuals think, feel, and behave in social contexts, including group dynamics, conformity, and attitudes. Students examine classic social psychology experiments and their real-world implications. This course complements the specialized Group Dynamics elective.
PSY ELEC 3 — Group Dynamics
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines how groups form, function, and influence individual behavior. Students study leadership, group decision-making, and intergroup relations. This elective builds directly on concepts introduced in Social Psychology.
PSY ELEC 4 — Introduction to Counseling
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: PSY (incomplete in source) Introduces basic counseling theories, techniques, and the helping relationship. Students practice foundational counseling skills such as active listening and rapport-building. This elective prepares students interested in counseling-focused career paths.
PSY ELEC 5 — Seminar in Psychology: Recruitment and Selection
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Focuses specifically on personnel recruitment and selection processes within organizations. Students learn to design and evaluate hiring assessments and interview procedures. This seminar extends the I/O psychology foundation into practical HR application.
PSY ELEC 6 — Seminar on Psychological Assessment
CAS | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Provides advanced, specialized study of psychological assessment tools and their application in professional practice. Students critically evaluate assessment methods across various contexts. This seminar builds directly on the foundational Psychological Assessment course.

COLLEGE OF BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION AND ACCOUNTANCY (CBAA) — Major: Marketing Management, 4 years
GEC 101 — Understanding the Self
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines the self through psychological, sociological, anthropological, and philosophical lenses. Students explore identity formation and how various disciplines frame human experience. The course encourages self-reflection as a foundation for personal growth throughout college life.
GEC 102 — Readings in Philippine History
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines Philippine history through primary source documents. Students learn to read and interpret original historical texts. This approach builds critical thinking skills that go beyond memorizing dates and events.
BAC 101 — Basic Microeconomics
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: BAC 104 Introduces microeconomic principles such as supply and demand, market structures, and consumer behavior. Students learn to analyze how individuals and firms make economic decisions. This course lays the analytical groundwork for later business and marketing subjects.
BAC 102 — Human Resource Management
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: BAC 103 Covers principles of recruiting, developing, and managing an organization's workforce. Students explore HR functions including staffing, training, and performance evaluation. It introduces the people-management side of running a business.
NSTP 1 — CWTS 1 / ROTC 1
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: (3) | Prerequisite for: NSTP 2 The Civic Welfare Training Service (or ROTC) component of the National Service Training Program. Students engage in community-based service or military training activities. It fulfills part of the mandatory national service requirement for college students.
GEC 103 — The Contemporary World
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines globalization and its economic, political, and cultural dimensions. Students analyze how global interconnectedness shapes local realities. The course encourages a broader, more critical view of current world affairs.
PATHFIT 1 — Movement Competency Training (MCT)
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite for: PATHFit 2 Basic movement competency and fitness training. Students learn proper form and baseline fitness assessment. This course lays the groundwork for the subsequent PATHFit courses.
GEC 104 — Mathematics in the Modern World
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Presents mathematics as a tool for understanding patterns, reasoning, and decision-making. Covers statistics, logic, and applied mathematical reasoning. The course is designed to make math relevant to everyday and professional situations rather than purely abstract computation.
GE 106 — Art Appreciation

GEC 105 — Purposive Communication
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Develops writing and speaking skills for academic and professional purposes. Students practice communication across varied genres and contexts. It prepares students for the kinds of writing and presentations expected in the workplace.
NSTP 2 — CWTS 2 / ROTC 2 / LTS 2

Students typically implement a community project or continue military/literacy training. This course completes the two-part NSTP civic service requirement.
BAC 103 — Good Governance and Social Responsibility
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: BAC 102 Examines ethical governance practices and corporate social responsibility. Students explore how organizations balance profit motives with social and environmental accountability. The course builds on HR management concepts to address organizational ethics more broadly.
BAC 104 — Business Law (OBLICON)
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: BAC 101 Covers the law on obligations and contracts as it applies to business transactions. Students learn the legal principles governing agreements, liabilities, and remedies. This course equips students with the legal literacy needed for later business operations coursework.
PATHFit 2 — Exercise-based Fitness Activities
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PATHFit 1 Continues the PATHFit series with structured exercise routines. Students track and improve personal fitness metrics. It builds directly on the movement foundations established in PATHFit 1.
GEC 107 — Science, Technology and Society
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines the interplay between scientific advancement, technology, and societal change. Students critically assess technology's ethical and social implications. The course situates business practice within a broader technological and societal context.
P.I. 100 — The Life and Works of Jose Rizal
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Studies Rizal's life, writings (Noli Me Tangere, El Filibusterismo), and his role in Philippine nationalism. Students analyze his lasting legacy in shaping national identity. The course is mandated for all Philippine college students under Republic Act 1425.
P.E 3 — Individual/Dual Sports Games
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 2 | Prerequisite: PE 2 Covers individual and dual sports such as badminton, table tennis, or similar activities. Students develop specific sport skills and strategies. This course continues the physical education sequence beyond the general PATHFit track.
GEC 108 — Ethics
CBAA Marketing Management | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Introduces ethical theories and frameworks for moral reasoning. Students apply ethical analysis to real-world dilemmas. It provides groundwork for later coursework on governance and social responsibility in business.
BAC 105 — Income Taxation

COLLEGE OF COMPUTER SCIENCE (CCS) — Major: Intelligent Systems, 4 years

CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Examines the self through psychological, sociological, anthropological, and philosophical lenses. Students explore identity formation and how various disciplines frame human experience. The course encourages self-reflection as a foundation for personal growth throughout college life.

CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Studies Rizal's life, writings (Noli Me Tangere, El Filibusterismo), and his role in Philippine nationalism. Students analyze his lasting legacy in shaping national identity. The course is mandated for all Philippine college students under Republic Act 1425.

Students practice discipline-specific Filipino registers. The course strengthens the ability to express technical and academic ideas in the national language.

Covers number systems, hardware/software basics, and the history of computing. This course establishes the baseline vocabulary used throughout the rest of the program.
ITEC 102 — Fundamentals of Programming
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 1, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: ITEC 103 Introduces programming logic and basic constructs such as variables, control structures, and loops. Students learn algorithmic thinking through hands-on coding. It serves as the entry point into the program's entire programming sequence.

CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Presents mathematics as a tool for understanding patterns, reasoning, and decision-making. Covers statistics, logic, and applied mathematical reasoning. The course is designed to make math relevant to everyday and professional situations rather than purely abstract computation.
GEC 103 — The Contemporary World
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 1, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines globalization and its economic, political, and cultural dimensions. Students analyze how global interconnectedness shapes local realities. The course encourages a broader, more critical view of current world affairs.

Covers arrays, functions, and file I/O. Students move from writing simple scripts toward more organized, reusable code.

Students learn to select appropriate structures for computational problems. This course forms the backbone of algorithmic problem-solving for the rest of the program.

CMSC 203 — Object-Oriented Programming
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Teaches OOP principles including classes, inheritance, polymorphism, and encapsulation. Students design modular, reusable software components. This course shifts students from procedural thinking toward object-oriented software design.
ITEC 105 — Information Management
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite for: CMSC 307 Covers database concepts including ER modeling, SQL, and normalization. Students design and query relational databases. These skills underlie most data-driven systems built later in the program.
MATH 24 — Calculus
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 1 | Units: 3 Covers differential and integral calculus with applications to rates of change and accumulation. Builds quantitative reasoning for later technical coursework. It supports later math-heavy topics in algorithms and machine learning.
GEC 107 — Science, Technology and Society
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Examines the interplay between scientific advancement, technology, and societal change. Students critically assess technology's ethical and social implications. The course situates computing within a broader societal context.
GEC 108 — Ethics
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Introduces ethical theories and frameworks for moral reasoning. Students apply ethical analysis to real-world dilemmas. It provides groundwork for later coursework on professional ethics in computing.
CMSC 204 — Algorithms and Complexity
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 | Prerequisite: CMSC 202 | Prerequisite for: CMSC 305, CSEL 303 Covers algorithm design paradigms (divide-and-conquer, dynamic programming, greedy methods) and complexity analysis via Big-O notation. Students learn to evaluate algorithmic efficiency and basic NP-completeness concepts. It is a gateway course for several advanced subjects later in the curriculum.
ITEC 106 — Applications Development and Emerging Technologies
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Focuses on building real-world applications using current frameworks and tools. Students apply prior programming knowledge to practical projects. The course also introduces students to emerging technology trends relevant to industry.
CSEL 301 — Introduction to Graphics and Visual Computing
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Introduces computer graphics fundamentals, including rendering pipelines and geometric transformations. Students work with basic graphics APIs. This course provides a technical foundation useful for later visual computing topics.
CSEL 302 — Introduction to Intelligent Systems
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 3 Introduces foundational AI concepts including search algorithms and knowledge representation. Students get their first exposure to machine learning basics. This course marks the formal start of the Intelligent Systems specialization track.
PATHFit 4 — Choice of Dance, Sports, Martial Arts, Group Exercise, Outdoor and Adventure Activities
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 2, Sem 2 | Units: 2 The culminating PATHFit course, continuing the student's chosen physical activity specialization. Emphasizes sustained fitness practice as a lifestyle habit. It completes the four-course PATHFit physical education requirement.
CMSC 305 — Automata Theory and Formal Languages
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 3 / Lab 0) | Prerequisite: CMSC 204 Covers formal language theory, finite automata, regular expressions, and Turing machines. Students learn the theoretical limits of what computers can compute. This theory underlies the design of compilers and language processors.
CMSC 306 — Computer Architecture and Organization
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) Covers CPU design, memory hierarchy, and instruction set architecture. Students gain a low-level understanding of how hardware executes software. The course bridges software-level concepts with the physical computing systems that run them.
CMSC 307 — Information Assurance and Security
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 2 (Lec 2 / Lab 0) | Prerequisite: ITEC 105 Introduces cryptography basics, network security, and risk management principles. Students learn to identify and mitigate common security vulnerabilities. It builds directly on the database concepts covered in Information Management.
CMSC 309 — Software Engineering 1
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite for: CMSC 311 Covers the software development lifecycle, requirements engineering, and UML modeling. Students learn structured approaches to planning software systems. It is the first of a two-course sequence in formal software engineering practice.
CMSC 310 — Networks and Communications
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: ITEC 101 Covers networking fundamentals including the OSI/TCP-IP models, subnetting, and routing. Students learn how data moves across distributed systems. This knowledge supports later coursework touching on distributed and networked applications.
CMSC 308 — Programming Languages
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: ITEC 103 Explores programming paradigms (functional, procedural, and declarative) and language design principles. Students compare how different languages solve the same problems. The course deepens the programming foundation built in earlier coursework.
CSST 101 — Advanced Presentation and Reasoning (IS 101)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) Develops advanced logical reasoning and technical presentation skills for the Intelligent Systems track. Students practice articulating complex technical ideas clearly. These skills are especially useful for defending research work later in the program.
CSST 102 — Basic Machine Learning (IS 102)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite for: CSST 103, CSST 104 Introduces supervised and unsupervised machine learning, covering regression, classification, and clustering. Students learn to train and evaluate basic ML models. It serves as the gateway course for the more advanced ML and NLP subjects that follow.
CMSC 311 — Software Engineering 2
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CMSC 309 Continues software engineering with a focus on testing, deployment, and project management. Students apply lifecycle concepts to a fuller development project. This course completes the two-part software engineering sequence.
CSEL 303 — Computational Science
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CMSC 204 Applies computational methods to solve scientific and mathematical problems. Students use programming and numerical methods to model real-world phenomena. The course builds directly on algorithmic foundations from earlier coursework.
CMSC 312 — Methods of Research
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 3 / Lab 0) Covers research design, literature review techniques, and methodology selection for computer science research. Students develop a formal research proposal. This course lays the groundwork for the undergraduate thesis sequence.
CMSC 313 — Human Computer Interaction (HCI)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 1 (Lec 1 / Lab 0) Introduces UI/UX design principles and usability evaluation methods. Students learn to design interfaces centered on user needs. It is a lighter, single-unit course focused specifically on design thinking.
CMSC 314 — Operating System
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) Covers process scheduling, memory management, and file systems. Students study how operating systems allocate and manage computing resources. The course also introduces disk scheduling strategies used to optimize storage access.
CMSC 315 — Social Issues and Professional Practices
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 3 / Lab 0) | Prerequisite: GEC 106 Examines ethics and professional responsibility specific to computing practice. Students explore case studies on technology's societal impact. It requires Art Appreciation as a prerequisite to provide a humanities grounding for the discussion.
CSST 103 — Natural Language Processing (IS 103)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CSST 102 Covers NLP fundamentals including tokenization, embeddings, and language models. Students learn how machines process and understand human language. This course connects the machine learning foundation to language-based AI applications.
CSST 104 — Advanced Machine Learning (IS 104)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Sem 2 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CSST 102 | Prerequisite for: CSST 105 Extends machine learning into deep learning and neural network architectures. Students build more complex models than in the introductory ML course. It serves as a required foundation for the robotics coursework that follows.
CSST 105 — Robotics (IS 105)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Inter-Semester | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CSST 104 | Prerequisite for: CSST 106 Covers robotics fundamentals including sensors, actuators, and embedded systems. Students design and build simple robotic control systems. This course applies machine learning concepts to physical, real-world hardware.
CMSC 501 — Undergraduate Thesis 1
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 3, Inter-Semester | Units: 3 (Lec 3 / Lab 0) Guides students through developing a formal thesis proposal, including research design and review of related literature. Students defend their proposed research direction to a panel. This course draws directly on the methodology skills built in Methods of Research.
CSST 106 — Perception and Computer Vision (IS 106)
CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 2 / Lab 1) | Prerequisite: CSST 105 Covers image processing, feature extraction, and object/face detection techniques. Students apply computer vision methods to real-world recognition problems. The course builds on the deep learning foundation from Advanced Machine Learning.

CCS Intelligent Systems | Year 4, Sem 1 | Units: 3 (Lec 3 / Lab 0) | Prerequisite: CMSC 501 Guides students through implementing, testing, and defending their completed thesis system. Students complete the full research-to-implementation cycle started in Thesis (description cut off in source; the rest is missing).

LSPU-LB PILLAR 4: SUPPLEMENTARY CAMPUS INFORMATION (plain-text version for RAG)
This section holds information supplied by the ontology maintainer (an LSPU-LB student, September 2026), text from the LSPU official website admission pages, and general safety guidance. It is NOT from the Student Handbook or the Citizen's Charter. Where a fact is unconfirmed, the block says so. Each block between the ===== lines is self-contained, so you can chunk by these blocks.

======================================================================
LSPU-LB CAMPUS: ADDRESS, MAIN PHONE, EMAIL AND OFFICE HOURS
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); campus map.
The LSPU Los Baños campus (LSPU-LB) is located in Malinta, Los Baños, Laguna.
Main phone: (049) 308-4530.
Main email: info@lspu.edu.ph.
Campus hours: 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM, Monday to Friday.
These are general campus details only. Office-specific numbers, emails and hours have not been supplied, and it is unknown whether individual offices (for example the clinic, library or guards) keep different hours. The main line is not an emergency line and is not answered after office hours.

======================================================================
WHO TO CONTACT IN AN EMERGENCY ON CAMPUS (SUMMARY)
Source: compiled from campus information supplied by the maintainer; 911 is general national information.
For a life-threatening emergency, call 911 (the national emergency hotline) and tell the nearest guard.
On campus:
- The Guard House at the main gate (on the left as you enter) for any security concern.
- The University Health Service clinic for medical help.
- The Guidance Office for an emotional or mental health crisis.
- Your College Dean for bullying or harassment.
The campus main line is (049) 308-4530 (Monday to Friday, 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM), but it is not an emergency line and it is not answered after office hours. For emergencies use 911 or a guard.
Only the campus main line is documented. Direct numbers for the guards, clinic and Guidance Office have not been supplied.

======================================================================
EMERGENCY CONTACT: NATIONAL EMERGENCY HOTLINE 911
Source: general national information (not an LSPU document).
911 is the Philippine national emergency hotline. Call it for police, fire, ambulance and other urgent emergencies anywhere in the Philippines, including on or near the campus. Call it for a crime in progress, fire, serious injury or any life-threatening situation, then inform the campus guard as soon as you can.

======================================================================
EMERGENCY CONTACT: CAMPUS GUARDS AND THE GUARD HOUSE (SAFETY AND SECURITY)
Source: campus map (location); Student Handbook (role of the Safety and Security Management Office); no phone number available.
For an emergency on campus, tell the nearest guard or go to the Guard House at the main gate (on the left as you enter). The Safety and Security Management Office handles security concerns.
Go to the guards for: fights, threats, suspicious persons, fire, accidents, and lost and found items.
No direct security phone number has been documented. The campus main line (049) 308-4530 (Mon-Fri, 8:00 AM-5:00 PM) is general, not an emergency line. For emergencies use 911 or go to the Guard House.

======================================================================
EMERGENCY CONTACT: UNIVERSITY HEALTH SERVICE (CAMPUS CLINIC)
Source: Citizen's Charter 2026, University Health Service, Service 1, p. 14; the 911 advice is general safety information.
The University Health Service (campus clinic) gives routine medical and dental consultation at no fee. Go there when you feel sick, have minor injuries, or need a first medical assessment on campus.
For a serious injury or life-threatening condition, call 911 and tell a guard rather than waiting for a clinic visit.
No direct clinic phone number has been supplied. The campus main line is (049) 308-4530 (Mon-Fri, 8:00 AM-5:00 PM); confirm the clinic's own hours.

======================================================================
EMERGENCY CONTACT: GUIDANCE OFFICE (EMOTIONAL OR MENTAL HEALTH CRISIS)
Source: Student Handbook Chapter 5, Article 1, Sec. 2 and the Mental Health Services articles; the 911 advice and the office location are from the maintainer and general safety information.
The Guidance Office (inside the OSAS building) offers counseling and mental health support to enrolled students. Go there for emotional distress, mental health concerns, and support after a bullying or harassment incident.
If you or someone else is in immediate danger, call 911 first.
No direct Guidance Office phone number has been supplied. The campus main line is (049) 308-4530 (Mon-Fri, 8:00 AM-5:00 PM).

======================================================================
EMERGENCY CONTACT: COLLEGE DEAN (BULLYING, HARASSMENT AND STUDENT WELFARE)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
Your College Dean should be told about bullying or harassment so it can be noted and solved quickly at the college level. Use it for reporting bullying or harassment quickly. See the block "HOW TO REPORT BULLYING OR HARASSMENT" for the full four steps. Dean office hours and contact details have not been supplied.

======================================================================
SAFETY GUIDE: WHAT TO DO IN AN EARTHQUAKE
Source: general earthquake-safety guidance, NOT an LSPU-LB protocol. The campus's official evacuation areas and drill procedure were not supplied, so none are stated. Ask the Safety and Security Management Office.
During shaking: DROP to your hands and knees, take COVER under a sturdy desk or table (or protect your head and neck with your arms next to an inside wall), and HOLD ON until the shaking stops.
Stay away from windows, glass, shelves and heavy objects. If outdoors, move away from buildings, posts and wires. Do not run out during the shaking.
After it stops: leave the building calmly by the stairs (not the elevator) to an open area, expect aftershocks, and follow the guards and faculty.
Help others only if it is safe, and report injuries to a guard or the campus clinic. Call 911 for serious injuries or fire.

======================================================================
SAFETY GUIDE: WHAT TO DO IN A TYPHOON
Source: announcement channels from the maintainer (September 2026); safety steps are general disaster-safety guidance, NOT an LSPU-LB protocol. Who decides suspensions and the campus's own typhoon procedure were not supplied here (see the Student Handbook rules on suspension of classes).
Before: check the official LSPU-LB Facebook page for campus-wide class suspensions, and your professor's Messenger group and Google Classroom for class-level announcements, before you travel. Keep your phone charged.
If you are already on campus in strong wind or heavy rain: stay indoors, keep away from windows and glass doors, stay out of open areas and away from trees, posts and loose signs, and follow the guards and faculty.
Do not travel through flooded roads or walk under fallen wires. Go home only when it is safe, and check announcements before coming back to campus.

======================================================================
HOW TO REPORT BULLYING OR HARASSMENT (STEP-BY-STEP)
Source: reporting to the College Dean is from campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The written complaint to OSAS and the committee are from the Student Handbook, Chapter 6, Articles 2 and 6 (Anti-Bullying Act of 2013, RA 10627, is the legal basis of the bullying offenses). Steps 1 and 4 are practical advice.
Bullying or harassment must be reported to your College Dean so it is noted and can be solved quickly. Four steps:
Step 1: Write down what happened. Note who was involved, what happened, when and where, and who saw it. For online bullying, keep screenshots and links. Do this while the details are fresh; it makes the report faster to act on.
Step 2: Tell your College Dean. Report it as soon as possible. The Dean notes the case and acts on it quickly at the college level; the Dean is also a member of the Student Discipline Committee for students of that college.
Step 3: File a signed written complaint with OSAS when formal action is needed. OSAS handles all complaints against alleged violators of the Code of Conduct. Complaints are made in writing to OSAS through the Coordinator of Student Discipline and must be signed by the complainant. Bullying is a Major Offense under the Code of Conduct.
Step 4: Get support. The Guidance Office offers counseling and mental health support to students affected by bullying or harassment. If anyone is in immediate danger, tell a guard or call 911 first. For gender-based or sexual harassment, the Gender and Development Office may also be approached (this referral is a suggestion; confirm with the office that it takes harassment reports).

======================================================================
STUDENT ORGANIZATIONS AT LSPU-LB (OVERVIEW)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); not from the Handbook or Citizen's Charter.
Campus-wide: the Supreme Student Council (SSC), the main student government.
Named organizations: Legion of Lures Esports (LURES), Psychology Society (College of Arts and Sciences), Ka-Peer Yu Organization (peer support, working with the Guidance and Counseling Unit), EsKultura (cultural and arts, with Himig EsKultura and Likha EsKultura), Ritmo EsKultura (official dance club), and Bay Breeze Group of Publications (campus publication).
Each of the 8 colleges also has its own student council, including the CCS Student Council (CCS-SC), and the colleges have their own student organizations. Full membership lists were not supplied.

======================================================================
STUDENT ORGANIZATION: KA-PEER YU ORGANIZATION
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The link to the Guidance and Counseling Unit is also reported on the university website (GCU and Ka-Peer Yu student-leader training, March 2026).
Ka-Peer Yu is a peer-support and student advocacy organization working with the Guidance and Counseling Unit. Its activities include mental-health awareness, peer advocacy, psychological first-aid training, and student development.

======================================================================
STUDENT ORGANIZATION: ESKULTURA (ESKWELAHAN NG MANLILILOK NG KULTURANG PILIPINO) AND RITMO ESKULTURA
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The 2026 recognition was supplied by the maintainer and not independently confirmed.
EsKultura is a cultural and arts organization that promotes Filipino culture through music, visual arts, dance, and other forms of artistic expression. It has groups such as Himig EsKultura and Likha EsKultura. LSPU named EsKultura its Most Outstanding Sub-Organization in 2026.
Ritmo EsKultura is the official dance club of LSPU-LB, associated with EsKultura. It performs different dance styles and represents the campus in cultural activities and competitions.

======================================================================
STUDENT ORGANIZATION: LEGION OF LURES ESPORTS (LURES)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
Legion of Lures Esports (LURES) is an esports and gaming organization at LSPU-LB. It is involved in organizing and participating in campus esports competitions. LURES was specifically featured in the 2026 February Fair esports competition.

======================================================================
STUDENT PUBLICATION: BAY BREEZE GROUP OF PUBLICATIONS (BBGP)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The Bay Breeze Group of Publications (BBGP) is a student publication that provides a platform for campus journalism, writing, news, and other student media.

======================================================================
STUDENT ORGANIZATION: PSYCHOLOGY SOCIETY (COLLEGE OF ARTS AND SCIENCES)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The Psychology Society is a student organization associated with the College of Arts and Sciences' Psychology program. It focuses on activities related to psychology, student development, and the academic community.

======================================================================
COLLEGE STUDENT ORGANIZATIONS (PER COLLEGE)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). Individual organization names were not supplied for these groups.
- College of Arts and Sciences (CAS): organizations serving students from programs such as Psychology and other CAS programs, covering academic, cultural, social, and student-development activities.
- College of Business Administration and Accountancy (CBAA): organizations for students in Business Administration and Accountancy, commonly focusing on business-related activities, professional development, competitions, and college events.
- College of Criminal Justice Education (CCJE): organizations for Criminology students, supporting academic, professional, leadership, and college activities.
- College of Fisheries (CF): organizations for Fisheries and related programs, supporting students through academic, professional, and college activities.
- College of Food Nutrition and Dietetics (CFND): organizations serving Food Technology, Nutrition and Dietetics, and related students, with activities connected to their academic and professional fields.
- College of Hospitality Management and Tourism (CIHT): organizations for Hospitality Management and Tourism students, usually involving hospitality, tourism, events, professional development, and college activities.
- College of Teacher Education (CTE): organizations serving students taking education programs such as Elementary Education and Secondary Education; they generally organize academic, professional, cultural, and student-development activities.

======================================================================
STUDENT COUNCILS AT LSPU-LB: SUPREME STUDENT COUNCIL AND THE EIGHT COLLEGE COUNCILS
Source: Student Handbook Chapter 5, Article 2, Sec. 3 (SSC definition); campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
Yes. All 8 colleges (CAS, CBAA, CCS, CCJE, CF, CFND, CIHT and CTE) have their own student council, and there is one Supreme Student Council (SSC) that governs them at the campus level.
The Supreme Student Council is the student body duly managed and elected at large by the students themselves; its President sits on the Student Discipline Committee and its Federated President sits on the Board. At LSPU-LB it is the main campus-wide student government: it represents students and helps organize campus-wide activities, programs, and student initiatives.
College councils: College of Arts and Sciences Student Council; College of Business Administration and Accountancy Student Council; College of Computer Studies Student Council (CCS-SC); College of Criminal Justice Education Student Council; College of Fisheries Student Council; College of Food Nutrition and Dietetics Student Council; College of Hospitality Management and Tourism Student Council; College of Teacher Education Student Council. The official abbreviations of the councils other than CCS-SC were not supplied.

======================================================================
STUDENT COUNCIL: COLLEGE OF COMPUTER STUDIES STUDENT COUNCIL (CCS-SC)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The 2026 Gawad Parangal recognition was supplied by the maintainer and not independently confirmed.
The CCS Student Council (CCS-SC) is the student organization representing students of the College of Computer Studies. It handles CCS-related activities, programs, and student representation. LSPU recognized CCS-SC as its Most Outstanding Mother Organization in the 2026 Gawad Parangal.

CAMPUS EVENTS AT LSPU-LB (SUMMARY)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). Event dates were supplied by the maintainer, are time-sensitive, and were not independently confirmed (status as of September 29, 2026).
Main events:
(1) ICAC-CAF, the sports and cultural competition among colleges. The 2026 campus level was held September 15-18, 2026, and the university-wide ICAC-CAF is scheduled for October 20-23, 2026 at the LSPU Santa Cruz Campus.
(2) The LSPU Founding Anniversary. The 73rd was celebrated with activities around November 26-28, 2025.
(3) College weeks: each college has its own festival week during the year, such as CCS Week and CTE Week.
Also, LURES took part in the 2026 February Fair esports competition.

======================================================================
EVENT: ICAC-CAF (INTERCOLLEGIATE ATHLETIC COMPETITION AND CULTURE AND THE ARTS FESTIVAL)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); dates not independently confirmed.
ICAC-CAF is the Intercollegiate Athletic Competition and Culture and the Arts Festival: mainly an athletic and cultural competition among the colleges. Sports include basketball, volleyball, chess, e-sports and swimming, among others, plus cultural competitions. Campus winners become the campus's representatives in the university-wide ICAC-CAF.
Editions in 2026: the LSPU-LBC (campus-level) edition was held September 15-18, 2026; the university-wide edition is scheduled for October 20-23, 2026 at the LSPU Santa Cruz Campus.

======================================================================
EVENT: LSPU-LBC ICAC-CAF 2026 (CAMPUS LEVEL)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). An LSPU-LBC ICAC-CAF 2026 is announced on the university website, but the dates were not independently confirmed.
The 2026 LSPU-LBC ICAC-CAF was held September 15-18, 2026. The colleges competed against each other in basketball, volleyball, chess, e-sports, swimming and other sports, plus cultural competitions. Afterward, the winners became LSPU-LBC's representatives in the university-wide ICAC-CAF. As of September 29, 2026 this campus-level event has already been held.

======================================================================
EVENT: UNIVERSITY-WIDE ICAC-CAF 2026
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); dates and venue not independently confirmed; schedules can change.
The university-wide ICAC-CAF 2026 is scheduled for October 20-23, 2026 at the LSPU Santa Cruz Campus. Each campus sends the winners of its own campus-level ICAC-CAF. As of September 29, 2026 this event has not happened yet.

LSPU celebrated its 73rd Founding Anniversary with activities around November 26-28, 2025. Dates for later anniversary celebrations were not supplied.

======================================================================
COSTS OF UNIFORM, SHIRTS AND ID LACE, AND A FIRST-YEAR BUDGET ESTIMATE
Source: prices reported by the maintainer in September 2026 and may change. The total is an estimate computed from those prices, assuming one of each item. Confirm whether the Php 500 PE price is per set.
All of these are bought at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).
- Uniform fabric: about Php 600-800 (fabric only, so you need a tailor to make the polo and pants, which costs extra).
- University shirt: Php 400.
- NSTP shirt: Php 350.
- PE T-shirt and shorts: Php 500.
- ID lace: Php 130.
- The official ID card itself is free for first-year students.
If you buy one of each item sold at the BAO, the total is roughly Php 1,980 to 2,180 (fabric Php 600-800 + University shirt Php 400 + NSTP shirt Php 350 + PE T-shirt and shorts Php 500 + ID lace Php 130).
Not included: tailoring for the uniform, books, OJT, transport and housing.
Laboratory costs are not an extra: they are already covered by the free tuition.

======================================================================
PRICE: OFFICIAL UNIFORM (FABRIC ONLY)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The official uniform costs about Php 600 to 800, depending on the fabric. The campus sells only the fabric, so you must find a tailor to sew the polo and pants; the tailoring cost is extra and is not included. The fabric is sold at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).

======================================================================
PRICE: UNIVERSITY SHIRT
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The University shirt costs Php 400 and is sold at the Business Affairs Office (BAO). It is worn every Wednesday.

======================================================================
PRICE: NSTP SHIRT
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The NSTP shirt costs Php 350 and is sold at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).

======================================================================
PRICE: PE UNIFORM (PE T-SHIRT AND SHORTS)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). Reported as one price for the PE T-shirt and shorts; confirm whether Php 500 is per set.
The PE T-shirt and shorts cost Php 500 and are sold at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).

======================================================================
PRICE: ID LACE (LANYARD)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
The ID lace costs Php 130 and is sold at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).

======================================================================
PRICE: OFFICIAL STUDENT ID CARD FOR FIRST-YEAR STUDENTS
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The Citizen's Charter lists Php 100.00 for ID processing without saying who pays it; confirm at the BAO.
The official ID card is free for first-year students and is obtained at the Business Affairs Office (BAO).

======================================================================
PRICE: ON-THE-JOB TRAINING (OJT) COST
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
There is no fixed amount. The OJT cost depends on where the student does OJT. Students choose their own OJT place, but the campus can also give students a list of companies it has connections with.

======================================================================
PRICE: LABORATORY COSTS (IS THERE A LAB FEE?)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
There is no separate laboratory fee: laboratory costs are already included in the tuition, and tuition is free for students covered by free higher education.
This applies to students covered by free higher education. Students who are not covered are assessed by the Accounting Office, and no laboratory-fee amount is documented for them. Consumable materials or supplies for a subject, if any, are not documented.

======================================================================
FREE HIGHER EDUCATION: WHAT YOU STILL PAY FOR (ADDITIONAL NOTE)
Source: Student Handbook Chapter 3, Article 2, Sec. 1.2-1.3 and Citizen's Charter 2026 Enrollment Step 2, p. 3 for the free-tuition rule; item prices and other costs are from campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
Free higher education covers tuition and other school fees for qualified bachelor's degree students. It does not cover students who do not qualify, students with RLE subjects, opt-out students, CTP/foreign students, and graduate students, who pay through the Accounting Office and Cashier.
Items you buy yourself are separate from tuition: uniform fabric (about Php 600-800, plus tailoring), University shirt (Php 400), NSTP shirt (Php 350), PE T-shirt and shorts (Php 500), ID lace (Php 130), OJT expenses, transport and housing.
Laboratory costs are not on this list: they are already included in the tuition, so a student covered by free higher education pays no separate laboratory fee.

======================================================================
LOST OR FOUND STUDENT ID: WHAT TO DO
Source: found-ID hand-in points from campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). ID processing: Citizen's Charter 2026, Business Affairs, Marketing and Production Office, Service 1, p. 13. Wearing the ID: Student Handbook Chapter 6, Article 3, Sec. 4. The lost-ID replacement fee and process were not supplied; confirm at the BAO.
If you LOST your ID, check the guard office and the student council first, because found IDs are turned in there. If it is not found, go to the Business Affairs Office (BAO), which handles student ID processing. The Citizen's Charter lists Php 100.00 for ID processing and the ID lace costs Php 130, but the replacement fee and steps for a lost ID are not documented, so ask the BAO.
If you FOUND an ID, hand it to the guard office (the Guard House at the main gate, on the left as you enter) or to the student council (the Supreme Student Council or your college's student council), who can return it to its owner.
The Handbook requires wearing the University ID at all times on campus (not doing so is a minor offense), so replace it as soon as you can.
Fee note: the Citizen's Charter lists Php 100.00 for ID processing, while campus information says the official ID is free for first-year students. The sources do not say which students pay the Php 100.00, so confirm at the BAO.

======================================================================
BUSINESS AFFAIRS OFFICE (BAO) AND ICT OFFICE: WHAT THEY HANDLE (ADDITION TO THE OFFICE DIRECTORY)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); campus map.
Business Affairs, Marketing and Production Office (BAO): Student ID, and the sale of the ID lace, uniform fabric, University shirt, NSTP shirt and PE uniform. The BAO is in the same building as the Clinic and Cafeteria, on the left side as you walk straight in from the main gate.
ICT office (inside the College of Computer Studies (CCS) building): activation of the student portal account.

======================================================================
GOOGLE WORKSPACE AT LSPU-LB (SCHOOL GOOGLE ACCOUNT)
Source: information from the project team and the maintainer that LSPU-LB mainly uses Google Workspace for study. The Student Handbook and Citizen's Charter do not describe it, except the Google Form used in Student ID processing (Citizen's Charter 2026, Business Affairs, Service 1, p. 13) and the Handbook's plan to use Google Classroom as the learning platform. Confirm the exact tools enabled and the account format with the university ICT office.
Google Workspace is Google's suite of online tools (Gmail, Classroom, Meet, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Forms, Calendar). It is mainly used at LSPU-LB for school-related activities: official school email, online classes and meetings, sharing and submitting coursework, group projects, forms and surveys, and schedules. Students use their school Google account to access these tools.
Account requirement: a school-issued Google account. Students request it inside the LSPU Student Portal once their portal account is active. The exact account format is not documented.
Because it is requested through the portal, activate your portal account first at the ICT office inside the CCS building. Confirm with your college which tools your instructors use.

======================================================================
GOOGLE WORKSPACE FUNCTIONS: WHAT EACH TOOL IS USED FOR
Source: general description of each Google tool and how the campus mainly uses it (maintainer and project team, September 2026).
- Google Classroom (online class management): join a class with a class code, read posted lessons and announcements, receive assignments, and submit outputs online.
- Google Meet (online meetings and classes): attend online lectures, consultations, and group meetings by video call.
- Gmail (school email): receive announcements from teachers, offices, and the college, and send school-related messages using the school Google account.
- Google Calendar (schedules and reminders): keep track of class schedules, deadlines, and school events, and receive reminders.
- Google Drive, Docs, Sheets and Slides (file storage and collaborative documents): store files in the cloud and write reports, spreadsheets, and presentations, alone or with groupmates, with shared editing.
- Google Forms (forms and surveys): fill out online forms, quizzes, surveys, and registrations. Example cited in the Citizen's Charter: Student ID processing starts by scanning a QR code or filling out a Google Form.

======================================================================
LSPU STUDENT PORTAL: ADDRESS, ACTIVATION AND REQUESTING THE SCHOOL GOOGLE ACCOUNT
Source: portal functions are from the Student Handbook Chapter 3, Article 5, Sec. 8.1.2 and the Citizen's Charter 2026 (Enrollment p. 2-3, Completion of INC/Removal p. 86-87, Enrollment Advising p. 90). The web address and the activation steps come from the maintainer (September 2026), not the Handbook or Charter. Confirm the ICT office hours and any requirements before relying on them.
The LSPU Student Portal is at myaccount.lspu.edu.ph. It is also called the Student Web Portal or the student portal.
Account requirement: an official LSPU student account. It is activated after the student is officially enrolled: visit the ICT office inside the College of Computer Studies (CCS) building and have your email and password verified.
Request your school Google account: once your portal account is active, you request your school Google account inside the LSPU Student Portal; the account is created from there.

======================================================================
ANNOUNCEMENT CHANNEL: CLASS MESSENGER GROUPS
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026).
Professors usually create a Messenger group for their class. It is the most frequently used way for students and professors to communicate: class cancellations, quiz announcements and other class information are posted there. Class-level announcements can also be posted in Google Classroom.

======================================================================
CAMPUS LOCATION: TRANSPORTATION TO THE CAMPUS (TRICYCLES) AND THE HALL
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer and the project team (September 2026); campus map.
Tricycles: tricycles are waiting outside the main gate, nearby along the road. The fare is about 20 pesos from nearby barangays; it costs more if you come from farther away, and you may need to take 2 rides. The 20-peso fare may change. Jeepney and bus routes and fares (for example from Calamba, the Los Baños poblacion or UPLB) are not documented.
The Hall: the Hall is the open space with a roof that you see when you enter the main gate. It is just inside the gate, on your right. It is an open covered space and a landmark used in many directions (for example "walk past the Hall").

WEBSITE ADMISSION RULE: FOREIGN STUDENT TEMPORARY STUDY PERMIT
Source: LSPU official website admission pages, text supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); not from the Handbook or Citizen's Charter (Foreign Students Admission: Study Permit). See also the Handbook's foreign students admission section above.
All foreign students must secure a temporary study permit from the ISS office before registering. The source does not spell out "ISS", and no LSPU-LB office by that name appears in the Citizen's Charter. Confirm the office with the Registrar.

======================================================================
WEBSITE ADMISSION RULE: TRANSFER WITHIN LSPU / SHIFTING OF COURSE (30-UNIT RULE)
Source: LSPU official website admission pages, text supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); not from the Handbook or Citizen's Charter (Transfer within LSPU / Shifting of Course). Compare with the Handbook shifting and transferring rules above.
A student who has earned at least 30 collegiate academic units and wants to move from one course to another files an application for transfer or shifting in the current college. It is referred to the accepting college together with a true copy of grades. If the accepting college approves, the student receives a permit to transfer and a copy of grades.
Shifting is not allowed without a valid reason. The student must qualify for the grade requirements of the department being entered. The application uses the prescribed form stating the reasons, with a copy of grades certified by the registrar. The College Dean must approve it, and it goes to the VPAA/Campus Director for approval, before 12% of the regular class meetings of the term have been held. A request submitted or received later is not considered for the following semester.
The website and Handbook rules overlap but are not identical: the Handbook asks for no failure above 6 units, the Registrar's shifting form, a recommendation letter from the previous Dean, Dean and Registrar approval, and completion of 60% of units at LSPU (75% for honors). The website adds the 30-unit minimum and VPAA/Campus Director approval. Confirm with the Registrar which applies.

======================================================================
WEBSITE ADMISSION RULE: TRANSFER FROM ANOTHER LSPU CAMPUS (WITHIN THE LSPU SYSTEM)
Source: LSPU official website admission pages, text supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); not from the Handbook or Citizen's Charter (Transfer Admission: Transfer within LSPU System).
Students from another LSPU constituent campus who have earned at least 30 collegiate academic units may be admitted as transferees, subject to the rules of the admitting college. The student must present honorable dismissal from the college or university last attended; must complete in LSPU at least 50% of the units required for the program (75% if running for honors); and the University's quota must not yet be filled.
An admitted transferee may not enroll in a subject whose prerequisite, taken elsewhere, has not been validated or repeated in LSPU. All courses for which advanced credit is sought must be validated at the time stated in the validation requirements.
The validation schedule was not supplied. The source says the transferee "must have completed" the 50% or 75% of units in LSPU; this is read as a residency requirement. Confirm with the Registrar. The 50% here differs from the 60% in the Handbook's college-to-college transfer rule, which covers a different situation.

======================================================================
FRESHMAN ADMISSION: ALS (ALTERNATIVE LEARNING SYSTEM) APPLICANTS
Source: note by the ontology maintainer; no ALS information appears in the Citizen's Charter, Admission Guidelines or Handbook.
The sources used for this assistant do not describe a separate route, document list or GWA rule for ALS applicants, so no ALS-specific rules can be stated.
What is documented for new freshmen: a report card (DepEd Form 138) or its equivalent (Form 137), good moral certificate, medical and other documents, the entrance examination and the interview, and the program's minimum GWA.
Ask the Guidance Office (entrance exam) or the Office of the Registrar which document takes the place of the report card for ALS applicants.

======================================================================
ADMISSION CUTOFF ADVICE: IS THE GWA CUTOFF STRICT? (ADDITION TO THE ANNEX A CUTOFFS)
Source: strictness and lower-cutoff advice from campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026); cutoff values are from the LSPU Admission Guidelines, 1st Sem AY 2026-2027, Annex A above; exam and interview from the LSPU website admission pages. Cutoffs may change each year.
The GWA cutoff is strict. If your GWA is below the minimum for the program you want, you cannot be admitted to it; your only option is to choose another program with a lower cutoff.
Example: a GWA of 86 qualifies only for the GWA-85 programs.
Cutoffs for 1st Sem AY 2026-2027: GWA 85 for the three BS Information Technology majors, BS Criminology and BS Hospitality Management (5 programs); GWA 87 for BS Fisheries, BTLE and BTVTE (3); GWA 88 for BS Psychology, both BS Business Administration majors and BS Tourism Management (4); GWA 90 for Accountancy, Computer Science, Food Technology, Nutrition and Dietetics, Physical Education, all five BS Education majors and Elementary Education (11).
Meeting the cutoff is not enough by itself: applicants must still pass the entrance exam and interview. CTE programs also need no grade below 85 in English, Math and Science, and CCS gives priority to STEM and ICT applicants.

======================================================================
LSPUCET (LSPU COLLEGE ENTRANCE EXAMINATION)
Source: campus information supplied by the maintainer (September 2026). The Citizen's Charter and Handbook call this the "LSPU Entrance Examination".
LSPUCET is the LSPU college entrance examination. It is the college entrance exam given by the Guidance Office to student-applicants, free of charge.

======================================================================
PRC LICENSURE EXAMINATION NAMES: FISHERIES AND FOOD TECHNOLOGY (NAME VARIANTS)
Source: checked by the maintainer against RA 11398, RA 11052 and PRC announcements (general public information, not an LSPU document). The Pillar 1 program blocks above use "Fisheries Professional Licensure Examination" and "Food Technologist Licensure Examination".
BS Fisheries: the official current name is the Fisheries Professional Licensure Examination, under RA 11398 (Philippine Fisheries Profession Act, 2019), which speaks of the licensure examination for fisheries professionals. The earlier law (RA 8550 / PRC Res. 2000-664) called the practitioners fisheries technologists, and PRC web pages still list the board type as Fisheries Technologists. Other names for the same exam: Fisheries Technologist Licensure Examination, Fisheries Technologists Licensure Examination, Fisheries Professionals Licensure Examination, and "Fisheries board exam".
BS Food Technology: under RA 11052 (Philippine Food Technology Act), PRC uses the plural "Food Technologists Licensure Examination", and 2026 PRC announcements call it the "Professional Food Technologists Computer-Based Licensure Examination". Other names for the same exam: Food Technologist Licensure Examination, Professional Food Technologists Licensure Examination, and "Food Tech board exam".
